User: What is the difference between a whale shark and a basking shark?
Joe: A whale shark is a carcharhinid and grows to over ten metres, feeding on a much wider range of prey including fish. A basking shark is a lamnid that reaches about nine metres and feeds almost entirely on plankton. The basking shark's gill slits are enormous, while the whale shark's are small and behind the head.

User: Why is the whale shark not dangerous despite its size?
Joe: A whale shark is a filter feeder with no functional teeth and no interest in large prey, so it does not bite. It does not deliberately target humans at all. The only real risk is being struck by the tail or hit by a boat when it is feeding at the surface.

User: What is the orca's effect on a whale shark population?
Joe: Orcas attack whale sharks, mainly at the caudal peduncle, and there are documented cases of a whale shark surviving with a scarred or partly amputated tail. A missing tail reduces swimming efficiency and therefore feeding ability. The predation pressure is low relative to the population, so it is a rare rather than a population-limiting cause of mortality.

User: What is the great hammerhead's diet?
Joe: A great hammerhead feeds on stingrays, which it detects with the ampullae of Lorenzini on its widely spaced head. It swings its head sideways to stun a buried ray before striking. Its sensory array also lets it hunt in murky water where vision would fail, which is why it is found in turbid coastal water.

User: How does a hammerhead detect a buried ray?
Joe: The ampullae of Lorenzini arrayed along the front of the cephalofoil detect the weak electrical field the ray produces. That signal passes through the sand, so the hammerhead can hunt prey it cannot see. The widely spaced eyes also give it a binocular overlap ahead of the head, which may help it judge the strike.

User: What is a thresher shark's unusual tail?
Joe: A thresher shark's upper tail lobe is longer than the rest of its body, and it uses it to herd fish into a tight ball. It then swings the tail through the school, stunning or injuring prey. The technique is unique to the family and makes threshers easy to identify from a distance.

User: How does a thresher shark herd fish with its tail?
Joe: The thresher swims alongside a shoal, using its long tail to corral the fish into a tighter ball. It circles the shoal, tightening it with each pass. When the group is sufficiently concentrated it lashes the tail through them, and it repeats that until enough prey is stunned to eat.

User: Why is a tiger shark's pattern useful?
Joe: A tiger shark's vertical bars fade as the shark grows, so an adult has faint stripes while a juvenile has sharp ones. The bars break up the outline against the dappled light of shallow water. The pattern also fades with age, which is why old tiger sharks look almost plain.

User: What does a tiger shark eat?
Joe: A tiger shark eats a very wide range of prey including fish, turtles, seabirds and marine mammals. It has a broad diet because it lives in a wide range of habitats from shallow reefs to open ocean. Its bite is powerful enough to break turtle shells and to bite through marine mammal skin.

User: Why is the great white shark's hearing so sensitive?
Joe: A great white detects sound from several kilometres, including a single gunshot, partly through the small opening on the underside of its jaw. That opening leads to the inner ear and is packed with sensory tissue. It lets the shark hunt by sound, which is how it finds prey in dark water or in fog.

User: How does the great white's jaw sense electricity?
Joe: Ampullae of Lorenzini cluster densely in the pores on the underside of the jaw, which is where a prey's field is strongest at close range. The shark can tell whether the thing in front of its mouth is alive by reading the field. That is how it detects an electroreceptive fish buried under the sand.

User: Why is the shark's electroreception useful for finding buried rays?
Joe: A ray buried under sand still produces a bioelectric field, and the shark's jaw pores detect it through the sediment. The shark then excavates the ray and strikes. It is the most famous application of electroreception in the marine world and it works in total darkness.

User: What is the shark's role as an apex predator on a reef?
Joe: A healthy population of large sharks suppresses the numbers of the smaller fish and invertebrates that they eat. That releases the smaller herbivores that keep the algae in check. The loss of that top-down control is one of the main reasons a heavily fished reef shifts to an algal-dominated state.

User: Why is a shark's fin made of cartilage an advantage?
Joe: Cartilage is lighter than bone and it is flexible, so a shark's jaw can absorb the shock of a hard strike without fracturing. It also allows the shark to grow large without the weight that a heavy skeleton would impose. The cost is a jaw that is less strong for its size, which is why a great white must build up speed and use its whole body.

User: What is a manta ray's filter feeding method?
Joe: A manta ray is a ram filter feeder like a whale shark, swimming forward with the mouth wide so water flows in and out past its gill rakers. The gill rakers on the underside of the gill arches strain out zooplankton and small fish. The reef manta can filter a very large volume per hour while cruising.

User: How large can a reef manta ray grow?
Joe: A reef manta can reach a wingspan of about seven metres, with a mature female appreciably larger than a male. A giant manta of the open ocean grows larger still. The females mature in their teens and live for several decades.

User: How does the manta ray's cephalic lobe work?
Joe: A manta ray has two cephalic lobes that it rolls forward and curls, which directs the plankton-rich water into the mouth. It uses them only while filter feeding and they are held flat while cruising. During courtship a male uses the same lobes to inspect a female's condition.

User: Why is the manta ray's black colour pattern useful?
Joe: The dark dorsal surface and pale ventral surface give the manta countershading, which cancels the light falling on it from above. The pattern also breaks up the outline. When seen from below, the white belly matches the bright surface of the water rather than showing as a dark shape.

User: What is the devil ray and how does it differ from a manta?
Joe: A devil ray has a much wider and rounder disc and a shorter tail with a barbed spine, while a manta has a wider cephalic lobe and a whip-like tail. Devil rays are also much smaller. The two are easily confused at a distance, which is a common source of misidentification for divers.

User: Why are manta rays now legally protected in many countries?
Joe: Manta rays grow slowly, produce very few young and were heavily targeted for their gill plates, which were sold as a folk remedy. Decades of harvest from small, slow-breeding populations drove a global collapse in numbers. Protection now covers most of their range after the CITES listings.

User: What is the sawfish's status?
Joe: Sawfish are among the most threatened fish groups in the world, with most species listed as critically endangered. They are caught in nets, and their rostrum is easily entangled, so a non-selective net loses the animal as well as the fish. The freshwater species in North America have almost disappeared.

User: Why is the smalltooth sawfish so rare in Florida?
Joe: The smalltooth sawfish was heavily fished for its rostrum and was also frequently caught and discarded in coastal nets. Florida was the last stronghold of the species. Recovery is now supported by federal protection, by a ban on retention, and by the slow natural growth of the surviving population.

User: What is the role of rays in a sandy seabed community?
Joe: Rays are important predators on a sandy bottom, eating crabs, worms and small flatfish. They disturb the sediment while feeding, which recycles nutrients and removes silt. Removing them changes both the prey populations and the physical structure of the bed.

User: Why is the electric ray's name appropriate?
Joe: An electric ray generates electric discharges strong enough to stun a fish or to cause a painful shock in a person. It hunts by discharging and detecting the resulting change in its own field. The electricity comes from a modified muscle organ rather than from the nervous system.

User: Why is the electric eel a solitary hunter?
Joe: An electric eel is a slow-moving ambush predator in turbid water, and it navigates and finds prey almost entirely by electrolocation. Its long body does not suit sustained chasing. It hunts alone, and its high-voltage discharge is a defensive and stunning tool rather than a schooling adaptation.

User: What is a ribbon eel and how does it feed?
Joe: A ribbon eel is a very slim, brightly coloured fish of the tropical Indo-Pacific that hunts small crustaceans with a long snout. It lives in a burrow in the sand and is often seen with only the head exposed. Its vivid colour makes it popular in the aquarium, although it is difficult to keep.

User: Why is the oarfish associated with a sea serpent legend?
Joe: An oarfish is a very long, ribbon-shaped deep-sea fish with a bright red fin and long pelvic rays, and it is rarely seen alive. When a decomposed specimen washes up, the long body and fin rays resemble a serpent. It is the origin of the sea serpent stories rather than any evidence of a real one.

User: What is a oarfish's actual diet?
Joe: An oarfish feeds on small crustaceans and fish, caught with a small mouth at the end of a long body. It lives at depths of several hundred metres, where it is a solitary predator. The larvae drift near the surface as long, ribbon-like forms before settling to depth.

User: What is the giant oarfish's red fin for?
Joe: The dorsal fin of an oarfish is bright crimson and can be raised along the body, which has been suggested as a display or a startle mechanism. Its function is not well established. The colour is unusual because most deep-sea fish are red or black and therefore effectively invisible.

User: Why is the coelacanth's lung vestigial?
Joe: The coelacanth descended from ancestors with a functional lung, and it retains a small lung-like organ that is filled with fat rather than air. It is not used for breathing. The vestigial organ is one of the pieces of anatomical evidence linking it to the tetrapod lineage.

User: What is a cusk-eel's habitat?
Joe: A cusk-eel lives on the continental slope at depths of a few hundred to over a thousand metres, on soft sediment. It is a bottom feeder on small invertebrates. Its deep distribution makes it poorly known and largely unstudied outside a few trawl surveys.

User: What is the grenadier and why is it important?
Joe: A grenadier is a deep-sea fish of the North Atlantic, most of which belong to the family Macrouridae with a tapering tail and a distinct dorsal fin. Several species are commercially fished, and their long-lived, slow-growing biology makes them highly vulnerable to overfishing. Their biology is largely unknown because they live at great depth.

User: Why are grenadiers vulnerable to overfishing?
Joe: A grenadier matures at around fifteen years, lives for seventy and produces relatively few young for its size. A fishery that removes the older, larger fish removes the bulk of the reproductive potential. Several North Atlantic stocks are now assessed as overfished for exactly this reason.

User: What is a grenadier's tapetum and why does it matter?
Joe: A grenadier's large eyes have a reflective tapetum that gives it excellent vision in the very low light of the deep sea. That lets it hunt at depths where almost no light penetrates. Its vision is unusually good for a deep-water fish and has been studied as a model for low-light vision.

User: What is the beaked red fish and what is unusual about it?
Joe: The beaked redfish lives in the North Atlantic and has a distinctive beaked snout. It was heavily fished and is now assessed as depleted, showing the pattern of a slow-growing deep-water species. Its case is a standard example of a stock that was fished before its biology was known.

User: What is the orange roughy and why is it so vulnerable?
Joe: An orange roughy grows very slowly, lives for over a century and first reproduces at around thirty years. Fishing the adults before that age meant no reproduction for decades, so the stock collapsed rapidly. It is the clearest case in the fisheries world of a stock fished on a biology nobody had measured.

User: Why are deep-sea species generally vulnerable to fishing?
Joe: Deep-sea fish typically grow slowly, mature late, live long and produce few young per year. Those are all the traits that make a stock slow to recover from overfishing. Because they are also found on seamounts and slopes that can be fished easily, the pressure reaches them faster than their biology can respond.

User: What is a seamount and why does it matter to a deep-sea fish?
Joe: A seamount is an underwater mountain, and it concentrates currents, nutrients and fish on and around it. The concentrated food supply supports far more biomass than the surrounding open ocean. That concentration is also what makes seamounts valuable to a fishery and therefore heavily exploited.

User: Why is the summit of a seamount biologically different?
Joe: The summit experiences stronger currents and more mixing, which can supply nutrients to the top of the water column. That supports an unusual community including filter feeders such as corals and sponges that are not found in the surrounding water. Those communities are slow-growing and easily damaged.

User: What is a sponge reef and why does it grow in deep water?
Joe: A sponge reef is built of three-dimensional glass sponge skeletons on a hard seabed in deep water, and the framework creates habitat for other animals. It grows slowly and only where there is a firm substrate to attach to. Its three-dimensional structure traps sediment and creates a community of its own.

User: Why are sponge reefs vulnerable to trawling?
Joe: A sponge reef is a brittle, slow-forming structure that a trawl's gear destroys outright. Recovery takes decades to centuries, since the framework itself is the habitat. Bottom-contact fishing on a known sponge reef is therefore among the most damaging fishing practices there is.

User: What is a cold-water coral and how does it feed?
Joe: A cold-water coral reef is built by stony corals in deep, cold water, and the corals feed on particles carried past them by the current. They grow far more slowly than their shallow-water relatives. Because the reef framework takes centuries to build, damage from fishing effectively removes habitat for a very long time.

User: Why do cold-water corals prefer carbonate rock?
Joe: A stony coral needs a firm carbonate surface to anchor to, and cold-water corals are therefore found on carbonate mounds and on limestone outcrops. Those hard seabeds are relatively rare in the deep ocean. Mapping where they occur is the first step in protecting the reefs from bottom contact fishing.

User: Why are cold-water corals found in Norwegian fjords?
Joe: Norwegian fjords have deep cold water, shallow shelf areas, carbonate rock and a supply of particles to feed on. Those are the conditions a cold-water coral reef needs, and the reefs are concentrated in the shelf break of the outer fjords. Warming and deoxygenation of the fjord water threaten them.

User: What is a wasp reef and why does it catch fish?
Joe: A wasp reef is a rocky reef with a current that accelerates through a gap, concentrating small fish into a narrow band where a bird or a fisher can take them. Sand eels gather there to feed on the plankton. It is a repeatable fishing spot, and it is also a sign of a healthy current system.

User: Why do sand eels gather at a current front?
Joe: Sand eels feed on the copepods that concentrate at a current convergence, and the flow also keeps their patch of sand stable. They form tight shoals in the tidal stream. Those shoals are the target of both birds and fishers, which is why the front matters more than the sand itself.

User: What is a sand eel's burrowing behaviour?
Joe: A sand eel burrows into clean sand to hide from predators and to rest, emerging at a current to feed on plankton. It leaves the sand in a leap, which is how fishermen spot a shoal. Its dependence on clean, mobile sand makes it vulnerable to any sediment disturbance.

User: Why are sand eels sensitive to sediment?
Joe: A sand eel needs clean, oxygenated sand of a particular grain size to burrow in. Sediment from land run-off both fills the interstices and reduces the oxygen in the bed. Burial is also lethal, so a sediment pulse can remove a shoal without killing any fish directly.

User: What is a sand goby's role in cleaning a shell-sand beach?
Joe: A sand goby eats the tiny invertebrates living in the interstices of the shell sand, which keeps the beach's animal community healthy. It also churns the sand as it feeds. It is a key part of what makes a shell-sand beach a productive habitat rather than a dead one.

User: Why is shell sand better than mud for a beach community?
Joe: Shell sand is coarse and permeable, so oxygen and water move through it and the invertebrates can live in the interstices. Mud is fine and anoxic in depth, so it supports only a surface community. That makes a shell-sand beach a nursery and a mudflat a feeding ground of lower quality.

User: Why is the mudflats' importance highest at low tide?
Joe: A mudflat is exposed only at low tide, and the exposure is what makes it a feeding ground for the birds and the fish that follow them. Waders probe the sediment for worms and shellfish. At high tide the flat is submerged and the food is inaccessible.

User: What is the effect of a sea wall on a mudflat?
Joe: A sea wall prevents the tide from reaching the mudflat behind it, so the intertidal community there dies and the sediment begins to erode. Without the wall, the flat would be regularly submerged. Hard engineering of a shoreline is one of the most reliable ways to destroy a coastal fish nursery.

User: Why does managed realignment of a sea wall help?
Joe: Managed realignment deliberately breaches or removes a sea wall so the tide can flood the land behind it. That restores the salt marsh or mudflat that the wall had excluded. The cost is the loss of the protected land, which is why it is controversial where the land is valuable for development.

User: What is the 'living shoreline' approach to coastal protection?
Joe: A living shoreline uses marsh, mangrove, oyster reef and dune vegetation to absorb wave energy instead of a hard wall. The habitat created is also a fish nursery. It can provide equivalent protection in low-energy settings, and it adapts as the shoreline moves rather than resisting it.

User: Why is an oyster reef a good breakwater?
Joe: A living oyster reef is a rough, permeable structure that dissipates wave energy by friction and by breaking the flow. It grows as the oysters reproduce, so it strengthens itself. It also provides fish and crustacean habitat, which a concrete breakwater does not.

User: What is the difference between a natural oyster reef and an oyster farm?
Joe: A natural reef is built by wild oysters on a firm substrate and can be hundreds of years old. An oyster farm is a set of racks or baskets suspended in the water, which grow a different and more uniform set of oysters. Reefs are far more valuable as habitat and as a breakwater.

User: Why are oyster reefs currently collapsing?
Joe: Oyster reefs have declined from historical levels through overharvest, disease and sediment deposition. The disease especially was the historic crash in Europe and is still a problem in North America. The loss removes both the habitat and the shoreline protection at once.

User: Why is the oyster filter feeding a threat to a nutrient-rich estuary?
Joe: An oyster filters a large volume of water, so it removes phytoplankton and its nutrients from the water. That competes with the other filter feeders and with the juvenile fish that depend on the plankton. Where oysters have been reintroduced at high density, they can suppress a plankton community they depend on themselves.

User: What is a fish's role in seagrass bed stability?
Joe: Fish grazing on algae and invertebrates in a seagrass bed keeps it clear and healthy, since algal overgrowth smothers the plant. Large herbivorous fish such as the dugong and the green turtle do the same work at a larger scale. Their removal contributes to the shift from a seagrass meadow to an algal mat.

User: Why is the dugong a keystone grazer in a seagrass meadow?
Joe: A dugong eats large quantities of seagrass, which stops the meadow being smothered by faster-growing algae and opens it for other grazers. Its droppings fertilise the sediment. Removing the dugongs allows the meadow to fail, which removes the fish nursery along with it.

User: What is the role of a grazing fish in a coral reef?
Joe: A parrotfish scrapes algae off the coral, which prevents the algae from outcompeting the coral for space. The scraping also exposes the bare carbonate the coral larvae settle on. Reefs with too few parrotfish, or with too many, both become algae-dominated, because the balance is what matters.

User: How does a crown-of-thorns starfish threaten a reef?
Joe: A crown-of-thorns feeds on coral polyps and can consume a reef's standing coral crop in a few years. A large outbreak can leave a reef white. Divers remove individuals by hand and the outbreaks are sometimes linked to warm water, which is a poor sign for coral survival.

User: What is a drupella snail and why does it matter?
Joe: A drupella is a coral-eating snail that multiplies rapidly on a reef and eats coral faster than the coral can recover. Unlike the crown-of-thorns, which is usually a predator on coral polyps in low numbers, a drupella outbreak strips entire reefs. It is one of the main non-predatory causes of coral loss.

User: Why is a coral reef's white skeleton a sign of bleaching?
Joe: A coral is an animal with symbiotic algae living in its tissue, and it gets most of its colour and much of its energy from those algae. When it expels them under heat stress, the white calcium carbonate skeleton shows through. Bleaching is therefore a loss of both colour and food supply.

User: What is coral spawning and when does it happen?
Joe: Many corals release eggs and sperm in a synchronised mass spawning, usually on a few nights after the full moon in a particular season. The timing is set by temperature and lunar cycles. The synchrony maximises the chance that eggs and sperm meet, which is critical in a sparse, dispersing population.

User: What is the significance of a mass coral spawning?
Joe: A mass spawning lets a reef broadcast gametes into the current on one night rather than producing gametes continuously, which is energetically far cheaper. It also concentrates the release, so the chance of fertilisation is much higher. Disruption of the timing by warm water is one reason recruitment has fallen.

User: Why is a coral's colour from the algae it contains?
Joe: A reef-building coral is largely transparent and its tissue is thin, so its colour comes almost entirely from the density of the symbiotic algae. Different corals host different densities and different species of algae. That is why a bleached coral does not simply change colour but goes white when the algae leave.

User: What is the competition between corals and algae for space?
Joe: A coral polyp grows very slowly and can only expand by budding new polyps at its margin. An alga can cover the same space in weeks. With adequate grazing, the coral's slow growth is enough to hold its ground, and without the grazers the algae win the space entirely.

User: Why are fish important to a reef's nutrient cycling?
Joe: Fish move nutrients between the water column and the reef by feeding above it and defecating on it. Their excretion supplies nitrogen and phosphorus directly to the corals. A reef with few fish therefore has a nutrient cycle that depends entirely on the water, which is a much less efficient arrangement.

User: What is a fish's role in cleaning a reef of algae?
Joe: The grazers include parrotfish, surgeonfish, tangs and some damselfish, each with a different grazing style and a different microhabitat. Together they keep the algal cover low enough for coral to grow. The composition of the grazer community matters as much as its total size.

User: What is the effect of a crown-of-thorns outbreak on the fish community?
Joe: Losing the living coral removes the three-dimensional habitat that reef fish shelter and feed in. Fish numbers and the number of species both drop. The reef does not necessarily recover when the starfish are gone, since the surviving corals may be the robust species that regrow more slowly.

User: Why is the Great Barrier Reef's fish community studied so carefully?
Joe: The Great Barrier Reef supports an enormous diversity of reef fish and is the largest single structure of its kind, so a change there is a large-scale signal. The monitoring programme tracks coral cover, fish abundance and cyclone damage over decades. It is one of the longest-running ecological monitoring datasets in the world.

User: What is the link between coral cover and reef fish diversity?
Joe: Reef fish diversity rises steeply with live coral cover because the coral provides the habitat complexity the fish live in. Where coral is replaced by rubble, sand or algae, the fish community is much poorer. That relationship is the reason coral loss is a fish loss and not only a structural one.

User: What is a parrotfish's role in producing carbonate sand?
Joe: A parrotfish bites off chunks of reef and passes them through its gut, where the organic material is digested. What comes out is fine white carbonate sand. On a healthy reef the parrotfish alone can produce a substantial fraction of the sand that forms a reef flat and an adjacent beach.

User: Why is a sea otter's kelp forest dependency relevant to fish?
Joe: A kelp forest depends on sea urchin grazing being controlled, and where the urchins are unchecked the kelp is gone to a barrens. The fish that live in the kelp lose their habitat, so the fish catch falls with the kelp. It is the clearest example of a cascade from an urchin to a fishery.

User: What is a kelp forest's role as a nursery?
Joe: A kelp forest is a productive, structurally complex habitat that shelters juvenile fish and provides feeding grounds. Many species spend their early life in it and move offshore as they grow. Loss of the kelp removes the nursery as well as the adult habitat.

User: What is the reason a sea urchin barrens forms?
Joe: A sea urchin barrens forms when the urchins remove the kelp faster than it can grow, usually following the loss of the predator that kept the urchin numbers down. The urchins then graze the holdfasts of the kelp and prevent any regrowth. It is a self-sustaining state unless the predator returns.

User: Why is a kelp forest productive?
Joe: Kelp is a large, fast-growing marine alga that produces a lot of plant material per unit area. It strips nutrients out of the water and shelters a dense community of fish and invertebrates. Its productivity per unit area is among the highest of any marine habitat, which is why it supports so much life.

User: How does a kelp forest hold onto the seabed?
Joe: A kelp anchors with a holdfast that grips the rock, and the stipe rises from it toward the surface. The holdfast is not a true root system, so it cannot take up nutrients from the sediment. It simply resists the wave force that would otherwise tear the plant off.

User: What is a bull kelp and why is it different from a giant kelp?
Joe: A bull kelp grows from the bottom on a single stipe and has a small canopy with several blades, adapted to rocky exposed coastlines. A giant kelp forms a floating canopy of many fronds at the surface. The two look quite different and they occupy different places on the same shore.

User: Why is a canopy kelp forest hard to restore?
Joe: A canopy kelp is a large plant with a long life that only reproduces sexually, so it does not simply regrow from a fragment. Recovery requires a healthy adult population producing spores over several years. Removing the predators and the grazers is not enough on its own, the forest has to come back from scratch.

User: What is the effect of a marine heatwave on kelp?
Joe: A marine heatwave raises the temperature beyond what the kelp's tissues tolerate and causes a bleaching-like loss and eventual die-off. A single severe summer can remove an entire canopy in a season. Repeated heatwaves then prevent the kelp from re-establishing, since it never gets a good growing season.

User: What is the role of a sea otter in a kelp system?
Joe: A sea otter eats sea urchins, which prevents the urchin numbers from removing the kelp. Where otters were hunted out, the urchins exploded and the kelp forests were destroyed. Their recovery allowed the kelp to return, which brought back the fish, the invertebrates and the fishery.

User: Why is the Southern Ocean kelp limited by ice?
Joe: The giant kelp of the Southern Ocean grows near the ice edge, where the water is cold and the ice keeps the grazers away. The ice scours the seabed and limits where the kelp can anchor. A decline in the sea ice has been followed by a decline in the kelp, which is the clearest polar-to-ecosystem link.

User: What is an icefish's diet?
Joe: An icefish feeds on krill, copepods, small fish and squid, and its broad diet reflects the productive polar water. Because it has no red blood cells, it is a slow, low-energy predator. That is adequate in the food-rich polar summer and is a liability if the prey distribution changes.

User: Why is the loss of sea ice a problem for an icefish?
Joe: An icefish depends on the productive water that forms under the ice and on the food web it supports. As the ice retreats and the water stratifies differently, the plankton production falls. The fish then has less food in the water where it now lives.

User: What is a krill's role in the Antarctic food web?
Joe: Krill is the link between the microscopic algae at the base and everything larger, including the whales, seals, penguins and fish that people eat. Krill swarms can be dense enough to be seen from space and are stripped by baleen whales. Managing the krill catch is therefore a question about the whole southern ocean fishery.

User: What is the biological carbon pump and how does a fish contribute?
Joe: In the biological carbon pump, plankton take carbon dioxide from the surface water and it sinks to the deep when they die or are eaten and defecated. Some carbon reaches the seabed and is buried. Fish shift carbon from the surface to the deep by feeding at the surface and dying or being caught at depth.

User: What is the daily vertical migration of the deep scattering layer?
Joe: Every night, fish, squid and zooplankton rise from the deep to feed in the surface layer, and they sink back at dawn. The movement is so synchronised that sonar operators named it the deep scattering layer. It is the largest daily movement of animals on the planet and it moves more carbon than most other processes.

User: Why do fish avoid the light when they migrate?
Joe: Staying in the dark by day makes a fish invisible to predators, which cannot see well at depth. The light level below the productive layer is low enough to be safe. The risk of being in the surface layer by day is greater than the value of the extra food that is not there anyway.

User: What is the oxygen minimum zone?
Joe: An oxygen minimum zone is a layer in the ocean where sinking organic matter is decomposed and the oxygen falls to a minimum. Many deep-water fish avoid it, so it forms an effective chemical barrier between the surface and the deep sea. As the zone expands, the deep-water habitat available to fish shrinks.

User: Why is the expansion of an oxygen minimum zone a threat?
Joe: As the zone expands it pushes the habitable depth upward, leaving deep-water species with less room and less food. Species that cannot move up lose their habitat entirely. It is one of the least reversible changes in the ocean because the recovery depends on the ocean's ventilation.

User: What is the role of a mid-water fish in the carbon cycle?
Joe: A mid-water fish feeds at the surface and carries the carbon down with it when it returns to depth or dies there. Large fish that migrate deep and die at depth are particularly efficient at transporting carbon. Removing those fish reduces that transport, which is one of the less obvious impacts of overfishing.

User: Why is a deep-water fish's body often gelatinous?
Joe: A deep-water fish has no need for a dense skeleton or strong muscles because there is little food and no predator to flee from. A watery, gelatinous body is cheaper to build and to maintain. It also makes the fish nearly invisible, since the body does not scatter much light in an environment that has almost none.

User: Why is the water pressure a problem for a deep-sea fish?
Joe: At several thousand metres the pressure is hundreds of atmospheres, which collapses any gas-filled structure. Deep-sea fish solve this by having no swim bladder and by keeping the body fluids close to seawater density. The problem is avoided rather than solved, which is why such fish cannot come to the surface intact.

User: What happens if a deep-sea fish is brought to the surface?
Joe: Its body fluids are adapted to a high external pressure, and lowering that pressure makes them comparatively less dense. The delicate cells and any gas-filled structures fail. A deep-sea specimen brought up is usually already dead, which is why they are studied almost entirely where they live.

User: What is the black smoker ecosystem?
Joe: A black smoker is a vent on the ocean floor where mineral-rich water at over 300 degrees erupts into cold seawater and precipitates as a black plume. Around it, chemosynthetic bacteria use hydrogen sulfide as their energy source. The whole food web is based on that chemistry rather than on sunlight.

User: How does a vent fish survive without sunlight?
Joe: A vent fish lives in a food web whose base is chemosynthetic bacteria that use the vent's chemicals for energy. The fish eat the bacteria and the animals that eat the bacteria. The system does not depend on photosynthesis at all, which is why it was discovered and was never expected.

User: What is a giant tube worm's relationship with vent bacteria?
Joe: A giant tube worm has no mouth and no gut. It houses chemosynthetic bacteria in a specialised organ, the trophosome, and supplies them with sulphide from the vent and with oxygen from its red plume. The bacteria feed the worm, which makes the symbiosis the whole of the animal's nutrition.

User: How does a vent community differ from one at a cold seep?
Joe: A cold seep releases methane and other hydrocarbons rather than hot mineral-rich water, and the temperature stays near ambient. The bacteria there use methane as their energy source. The community looks similar in structure but the chemistry and the species are different, since the seep is a much gentler environment.

User: Why is a hydrothermal vent community dependent on the vent itself?
Joe: The bacteria at a vent need a continuous flow of sulphide to fuel their metabolism. If the vent stops, the whole food web collapses within a short time. That dependence on a localised chemical resource is what makes a vent community so small in area and so vulnerable to any disturbance.

User: What is a hadal snailfish and where does it live?
Joe: A hadal snailfish lives in the hadal trenches of the deep ocean, in trenches deeper than about six thousand metres. It is one of the deepest-living fish known and has no swim bladder. The pressure at that depth is around six hundred atmospheres and it tolerates a very narrow temperature range.

User: Why is a hadal snailfish limited to trenches?
Joe: A hadal snailfish depends on the very high pressure of a trench and on the specific prey found there. It cannot survive at shallower depths where the pressure is lower, so it is confined to the trench system. Its range is therefore a set of isolated patches rather than a continuous belt.

User: What is a fish's adaptation to a trench's food supply?
Joe: A hadal snailfish eats the amphipods and other crustaceans that are abundant in a trench, where the funneling of organic matter concentrates them. Its large mouth and expandable stomach let it take a large meal when one is available. It is an ambush predator that can go a long time between meals.

User: What is a bristlemouth and why is it so numerous?
Joe: A bristlemouth is one of the most abundant vertebrates on Earth, forming huge aggregations in the deep ocean. It is tiny, has no swim bladder and produces very little of its own light. Its success is based on being everywhere in enormous numbers, which is what makes it food for almost everything else.

User: What is a snailfish's lack of a swim bladder about?
Joe: A snailfish has no swim bladder because the pressure at depth would collapse a gas-filled organ. It instead relies on its oily liver and on the low density of its gelatinous body. The cost is that it has to keep swimming or hover rather than saving energy by holding depth with gas.

User: What is the percentage of a fish's brain used for smell?
Joe: In some deep-sea and nocturnal fish, the olfactory bulbs occupy a large proportion of the brain, in one case around two thirds of it. That reflects how much of the animal's behaviour depends on chemical cues. Vision is reduced in the same fish, so the two patterns are complementary.

User: Why do deep-sea fish have small eyes?
Joe: In the deep ocean below about a thousand metres there is effectively no light, so an eye cannot gather any information. A deep-sea fish therefore reduces or loses its eyes entirely. Species that migrate up to the light and back down again keep their eyes, since they use them every night.

User: What is the difference between a lanternfish and a bristlemouth?
Joe: A lanternfish performs the nightly vertical migration and has a well-developed eye with a large lens for the low light. A bristlemouth does not migrate, has small eyes and lacks the developed visual system. A lanternfish is a visual feeder in dim water; a bristlemouth is a chemical-feeling drifter.

User: What is the function of a lanternfish's ventral photophores?
Joe: The ventral photophores on a lanternfish produce light that matches the light coming down from above, erasing the fish's silhouette. It also uses them to signal to a potential mate at a specific depth. The pattern is species specific, which makes it a reliable identification character in a very uniform-looking group.

User: Why is a hatchetfish's photophore pattern on the underside?
Joe: A hatchetfish's photophores are on the ventral surface because the light to be cancelled comes from above and the silhouette is seen from below. A predator looking up sees light from the surface rather than a dark shape. It is counter-illumination at its most literal, and it only works in the twilight zone.

User: What is the range of the twilight zone?
Joe: The twilight zone, or mesopelagic, runs from roughly two hundred to about a thousand metres, where some light still penetrates from the surface. Below that is the midnight zone, with no light at all. The mesopelagic layer is where the daily vertical migration happens and where the counter-illumination fish live.

User: Why is the mesopelagic layer the largest habitat by volume?
Joe: It runs from a few hundred to a thousand metres over the whole ocean, which is by far the greatest volume of any habitat on Earth. The fish in it are correspondingly abundant. Much of the deep-water fish catch that is compressed into a few species per tonne comes from this layer.

User: What is a lanternfish's oil content and why is it high?
Joe: A lanternfish stores a large fraction of its body mass as wax esters, a dense lipid that provides energy for the nightly migration. The same lipids make it buoyant enough to rise without much swimming. That is why lanternfish meal is a common aquaculture feed ingredient and why the fishery for them is tightly regulated.

User: What is the risk of overfishing a mesopelagic fish?
Joe: Mesopelagic fish are slow to mature, grow slowly and live long, so they can be overfished without an obvious collapse in the short term. They are also the base of the food web for larger predators and seabirds. That risk is why many of these fisheries are managed as experimental and are being researched before expansion.

User: What is a hatchetfish's deep body shape for?
Joe: A hatchetfish is very thin and deep-bodied, like a disc with a keel, which puts its eyes on the upward-facing part of the body. That lets it look up through its own body for prey silhouetted against the light. Its silvered ventral surface then counter-illuminates so predators looking up see nothing.

User: What is the reflectance of a hatchetfish's underside?
Joe: The ventral surface is covered with photophores whose output is matched to the downwelling light. Measurements in the field have found the match to be accurate within a few percent across a range of depths. It is a rare case of an animal that is reproducing an external light source well enough to disappear.

User: What is a dragonfish's red searchlight?
Joe: A dragonfish has red photophores it can shine forward, because most deep-sea prey cannot detect red light. It uses the beam to find prey that is not avoiding red, which none of them are. It also has a sensitive red-sensitive retina tuned to the frequency of the light it produces.

User: Why can an unarmoured deep-sea fish be successful?
Joe: A deep-sea fish such as a hatchetfish or a snailfish faces almost no predation pressure in a dark environment with few large predators. Without that pressure, a heavy armour and a strong skeleton are unnecessary, and energy goes into reproduction instead. The low-pressure environment permits a body that would not survive elsewhere.

User: What is a larvacean and why is it important to a carbon cycle?
Joe: A larvacean is a small tunicate that builds a mucus house and filters particles out of the water. It is abundant in the mesopelagic and contributes to the biological carbon pump by repackaging the sinking particles into denser faecal pellets. Those pellets sink far more efficiently and carry carbon into the deep ocean.

User: What is a discarded larvacean house and why does it matter?
Joe: A larvacean abandons its mucus house daily, and the abandoned houses carry the filtered particles down as they sink. They aggregate and are eaten by sinking organisms, which repackage them again. The daily cycle of a larvacean is one of the more efficient particle-pumping systems in the ocean.

User: What is a siphonophore and is it a single animal?
Joe: A siphonophore is a colonial animal made of specialised individuals, each specialised in swimming, feeding or reproduction, joined in a colony. The Portuguese man o' war and the giant siphonophore are examples. It looks like one animal with many bodies but is genetically a colony of cloned individuals.

User: What is the giant siphonophore's length?
Joe: A giant siphonophore colony of the genus Praya has been recorded at over forty metres, which makes it among the longest animals known. It is a drifting colony of linked zooids in deep water. It is not a single body, which is why it is classified in the cnidarian group rather than among the fish.

User: What is a salp and why are they numerous?
Joe: A salp is a barrel-shaped tunicate that filter feeds on the mesopelagic and forms huge seasonal blooms. They reproduce asexually in a chain when conditions are good, so the population grows very fast. They graze on the same particles the mesopelagic fish do and compete with them directly.

User: What is a pyrosome and what makes it glow?
Joe: A pyrosome is a colonial tunicate that forms a hollow tube of linked individuals and is one of the few large organisms that emits light. Light is released as a wave down the colony when it is disturbed, so the whole thing lights up at once. The function is thought to be to attract a predator that will eat the colony and take the light with it.

User: Why does a comb jelly look like a rainbow?
Joe: The beating of the ctene's comb plates splits and diffracts the light, which creates the moving rainbow bands along its rows. The colours are produced by the same physics as in a diffraction grating. It is not bioluminescence, since the light is scattered ambient light and not emitted.

User: What is a Venus girdle and why does it glow?
Joe: A Venus girdle is a ctenophore that forms a long ribbon and emits a travelling wave of blue-green light along its comb rows when disturbed. The light is produced by a luciferin-luciferase system in the tissue beneath the rows. It is one of the largest bioluminescent displays known.

User: What is the effect of light pollution on a coastal fish?
Joe: A coastal fish's eggs and larvae in the surface layers can be attracted to an artificial light and held there rather than carried to the open sea. They also suffer a higher predation rate in the illuminated area. The result is a lower recruitment near a lit coast than at a comparable dark one.

User: Why are hatchery-reared fish a poor replacement for wild juveniles?
Joe: A hatchery fish has usually never seen a predator, has grown at a uniform rate with abundant food, and has learned nothing about the river. Its survival in the wild is much lower than that of a wild fish of the same size. Its release also dilutes the local genetic stock, so the two effects compound.

User: What is the natural mortality of a released hatchery fish?
Joe: Most of a hatchery cohort is gone within days to weeks after release, killed by predation, by the unfamiliar conditions and by poor condition. A small fraction survives to return as an adult. The number of adult returns per fish released is typically a very small fraction of one percent.

User: Why does a hatchery need an adequate number of wild broodfish?
Joe: A hatchery taking all its brood from a small number of wild fish reduces the genetic diversity of the stock's contribution to the next generation. Genetic monitoring has shown that a hatchery run from a handful of parents measurably shifts the allele frequencies of a wild population. A quota on broodfish is the usual safeguard.

User: What is an 'effective population size'?
Joe: It is the size of an ideal population that would lose genetic diversity at the same rate as the real one. It is usually much smaller than the census count because of unequal family size, a skewed sex ratio and the loss of a few generations. It is the measure that matters for the long-term viability of a stock.

User: Why does a skewed sex ratio reduce a fish population?
Joe: If the effective number of breeding individuals of one sex is small, the effective population size falls sharply and inbreeding rises. In a hatchery that happens easily, since one large female can contribute most of the eggs. Managing the sex ratio and the broodstock size is a standard part of hatchery operation.

User: What is a 'bottleneck' in a fish stock?
Joe: A bottleneck is a sharp reduction in population size that leaves the survivors' genes as a random and unrepresentative sample of the original. Genetic diversity falls and can stay low even as numbers recover. Every stock that collapsed in the twentieth century went through a bottleneck, and the diversity loss outlasts the numerical recovery.

User: What is a 'homing error' in a returning salmon?
Joe: A homing error is a returning salmon that enters the wrong river system and therefore fails to spawn. The rate is normally low and is measured by analysing the otolith's strontium signature on the fish as they are caught. An unusually high error rate usually means the river's chemical signature has changed or a dam has confused the navigation.

User: What is a stream temperature refuge for a salmon?
Joe: A cold-water refuge is a spring-fed channel or a shaded reach that stays cool even when the main river warms. Surviving adult salmon hold in it through the summer and do not die of heat stress. Losing the riparian shade that creates the refuge is one of the more serious consequences of clearing a riverbank.

User: What is the effect of a flood on a spawning gravel?
Joe: A large flood scours the gravel and washes the eggs out of it. Moderate flows are fine and even beneficial, since they keep the gravel clean of silt. It is the extreme that destroys the eggs, so the frequency of the extreme events is the relevant measure for the population.

User: Why is a clownfish immune to sea anemone stings?
Joe: Its mucus lacks the substances that trigger the anemone's stinging cells, and it is coated by the anemone's own mucus. The fish also has thick skin and behaviour that avoids triggering the nematocysts. A clownfish that stops moving among the tentacles is attacked, which is why anemonefish have to keep swimming.

User: What is the mutualism between a clownfish and a sea anemone?
Joe: The anemone gains a defender that drives off anemone-eaters, and it gains scraps of food from the fish. The fish gains a predator-proof shelter where its eggs can be laid. Both partners lose badly if the other is removed, which is what makes it a true mutualism rather than a convenient arrangement.

User: Why is an anemonefish called a false clownfish?
Joe: The subfamily of damselfish includes the true clownfish, and the anemonefishes are their close relatives. The name distinguishes them from the true clownfish, which do not live among anemones. Both groups live around reefs, so the distinction is mostly taxonomic rather than behavioural.

User: Why does a tang need a long, narrow body?
Joe: A surgeonfish grazes algae off the rock with a beak-like mouth as it moves along the reef. The narrow profile lets it work into crevices that a deeper-bodied fish cannot reach. It also grazes constantly, which is why the species needs a long swim along the reef and access to a large area of rock.

User: Why is a tang's surgeon-like tail spine a real weapon?
Joe: The modified scale at the base of the tail is sharp and is normally folded into the flank. The fish swings the tail sideways when threatened, and the spine can cause a deep wound in a human hand. The species is named for that resemblance to a surgical instrument.

User: What does a wrasse do on a reef?
Joe: Many wrasses are active predators that follow other fish and take their prey, and some pick parasites off the skin of larger fish. The cleaner wrasses are the best known of that group and set up a cleaning station that other fish visit. That cleaning is a real service, although the wrasse eats the parasite along with some mucus.

User: Why is a Napoleon wrasse valuable on a reef?
Joe: A large Napoleon wrasse eats the urchins and the algae that would otherwise overgraze a reef, so its presence raises coral cover. It also grows large and is long-lived, which makes it a slow-recovering loss from a fishery. A fish of that value is a target, which is why it is protected in many places.

User: Why does a grouper change colour?
Joe: A grouper has pigment cells it can control, so it can darken, lighten or pattern itself in seconds. It uses that to blend against the rock and to signal mood during a court. The same ability makes a market grouper easy to price by appearance, and the darkening reflects stress in a captured fish.

User: Why does a grouper inflate itself when threatened?
Joe: A grouper gulps water into the mouth and the gut, which makes its body too large for a predator to handle. It can also wedge itself into a crevice and swell there. The behaviour is a defence rather than a way of controlling buoyancy, and it is a common sight on a reef when a fish is first speared.

User: Why is a giant grouper's status a conservation concern?
Joe: An adult giant grouper can weigh well over a hundred kilograms, grows very slowly and matures only at large size. Every fish taken is therefore many years of lost reproduction. Its numbers dropped sharply with the live reef-fish trade before protective measures took effect.

User: Why does a butterflyfish's narrow snout matter?
Joe: A butterflyfish picks individual polyps and small invertebrates out of the reef, which is a job that needs a fine, pointed mouth. It has to reach into cracks without touching the coral. That diet also makes it difficult to breed, and it has to be fed carefully in a tank.

User: Why are many butterflyfish pairs hard to keep together?
Joe: A pair needs a long tank, and the two fish have to agree to pair, which many do not. An unpaired individual may live alone, but a rejected partner is harassed constantly. Keeping two does not guarantee breeding, and it can make the aggression worse rather than better.

User: Why is a triggerfish's name accurate?
Joe: A triggerfish braces its second dorsal spine against the substrate, and the first spine is then locked against it. Only when the fish relaxes does the trigger release. That locking mechanism is what allows the fish to wedge into a crevice and resist being pulled out by a predator.

User: Why is a triggerfish hard to keep?
Joe: It is a persistent grazer that will bite anything it decides is in the way, including a hand and a thermostat cable. It also needs a well-lit tank with algae growth to keep it occupied. A starved triggerfish becomes destructive, which is why algae supply matters more than the fish looks.

User: Why does a pufferfish inflate?
Joe: It gulps water and air into a stretchable stomach, which raises its apparent size beyond a predator's gape. As a second line of defence it can release a toxin. A puffer that has inflated a predator too large to swallow often survives to deflate and escape.

User: Why is a pufferfish's poison concentrated in its organs?
Joe: The toxin is tetrodotoxin, which the fish accumulates from the organisms it eats, chiefly certain bacteria and molluscs. It is stored in the liver, ovaries, skin and intestines. The flesh of many species is therefore safe to eat while the organs are not, which is why preparation has to be done by someone who knows the species.

User: Why is the fugu preparation a licensed trade in Japan?
Joe: The toxin is potent enough to kill a person from a small amount, and a mistake in removing the organs is fatal. A licensed practitioner is required to cut the fish, and the shape of the served slices is the cut that survives the ritual. The licensing exists because the preparation cannot be improvised safely.

User: Why is a piranha's reputation mostly wrong?
Joe: Most piranha species are omnivores that eat fruit, seeds and scavenged material as much as they eat fish. A school of healthy adults in a natural river causes no threat to a swimmer. The dangerous cases are rare and usually involve a fish in poor condition, which then bites at anything available.

User: Why do piranha schools attack in numbers?
Joe: An individual fish rarely wins a fight with a larger one, so a group gives each member a better chance of feeding. The sound and movement of the group triggers more fish to join, which is a form of social facilitation. The famous feeding frenzy is that behaviour amplified on a broadcast channel rather than an attack plan.

User: Why is the red-bellied piranha the one most often exported?
Joe: It is one of the most commonly farmed and traded piranha, and it is also among the least aggressive. Its red belly and its hardiness in captivity made it the species aquarists wanted. The trade therefore focused on a species that does not justify the reputation of the group.

User: Why is an arapaima a giant among freshwater fish?
Joe: An arapaima is one of the largest freshwater fish anywhere, reaching well over two metres and over a hundred kilograms in the Amazon. It is a member of the arapaima group, close to the bonefish and tarpon. That size makes it one of the most valuable freshwater catches in the world.

User: Why is an arapaima's respiration partly aerial?
Joe: It has to come to the surface to gulp air, because the water it lives in is often deoxygenated and the gills alone cannot supply enough. It is therefore found near the surface, and the habit is distinctive. The same habit means it is easy to catch with a net at dawn.

User: Why is an arapaima protected in much of the Amazon?
Joe: It grows slowly, matures late and produces relatively few eggs for its size, so a fishery can remove the population's future very quickly. Regulation and closed seasons were introduced because the head count fell sharply after the aquarium and food trades. A large fish that takes years to replace is easy to overfish.

User: Why is a discus fish hard to keep?
Joe: A discus needs very soft, very warm, very clean water and a steady temperature day and night. It also demands large frequent water changes, because it eats protein-rich food and produces a lot of waste. Its care is unusually unforgiving, which is why experienced keepers value it despite that.

User: Why does a discus eat its own young when stressed?
Joe: A discus is a mouthbrooder that holds its fry in its mouth for about three weeks. A frightened or startled parent swallows them, which is a built-in response to a threat rather than cruelty. Keeping the pair undisturbed and the water stable is what prevents the loss.

User: Why is the blue discus different from the green discus?
Joe: The blue discus comes from the central Amazon near Manaus, in blackwater habitats, and it is generally hardier than the green form. The green discus comes from clearer, harder-water areas of the lower Amazon. That difference in origin is why hybrids of the two are not straightforward to keep.

User: Why does a pleco need wood in a tank?
Joe: A pleco rasps the wood for the biofilm and for the invertebrates that live in it, which is part of its natural diet. A bare tank with plastic plants gives it nothing to do. Driftwood therefore serves both a nutritional and a behavioural purpose for that fish.

User: Why is a convict cichlid named for its markings?
Joe: Its body carries dark vertical bars like a prison uniform, which is the origin of the common name. The bars are a pattern that breaks up its outline among plants and rocks. It is a hardy fish that tolerates water many of its relatives cannot, and it breeds readily.

User: Why do many cichlids show parental care?
Joe: A cichlid pair defends a patch of substrate, lays eggs on it, and both parents guard the brood. In a species that hides its young in the substrate, that care is what lets a few offspring survive. The behaviour also makes the fish territorial, since a parent treats anything near the brood as a threat.

User: Why is a mouthbrooding cichlid named for its method?
Joe: The female, or in some species the male, takes the eggs or the fry into the mouth and holds them there for days. The parent does not eat during that period and turns the brood over to keep it clean. It is an unusual and effective form of care for fish that live in exposed water.

User: What is a convict cichlid's tolerance of different water?
Joe: It tolerates a wide range of hardness and a moderate pH, which is one reason it is such a common aquarium fish. It does need clean water and a stable temperature. That tolerance is also the reason it has established itself well outside its native range.

User: Why is a koi a domesticated carp?
Joe: The koi is a coloured variety of the common carp, selected over centuries for colour, pattern and body shape. Its wild ancestor is the carp of rivers and lakes across Eurasia. Every koi carries the genes of that wild fish, so its needs remain close to the original species.

User: Why does a koi jump out of a pond?
Joe: Low water quality, a sudden change in the weather, spawning behaviour, and a water temperature that is too warm all trigger jumping. A koi that lands on the grass usually survives if it is put back and kept wet. Most losses are from a jump into a dry, sunlit place, so a net over the pond is the real protection.

User: What is a koi's swim bladder problem?
Joe: A swim bladder disorder shows as a fish that floats, sinks, spins, or holds itself at an angle. It is usually caused by poor water quality, a bacterial infection, or a physical injury. It is treatable in many cases by improving the water and adding salt, but a deformed buoyancy from an early infection is permanent.

User: Why do goldfish colours fade in a dark tank?
Joe: The pigment comes from the fish's diet, and the colour deepens in good light and with a varied food. In a dark or poorly lit tank a goldfish goes pale over time. That is a lighting and nutrition problem rather than a sign of a sick fish, and it is reversible to a degree.

User: Why do some goldfish develop a bent spine?
Joe: A swim bladder problem in a young fish distorts the vertebrae as the fish grows, which produces the bent or shortened body seen in fancy varieties. Repeated low-level water quality problems during growth are the usual cause. It is one reason the round-bodied varieties need better water than an ordinary goldfish.

User: Why is the hump on a fancy goldfish's head?
Joe: The wen is a growth of skin over the head that enlarges as the fish grows, and it hides deformed skull bones underneath. Fancy varieties with a large wen are the result of selective breeding for that shape. A very heavy wen can obstruct the mouth and sight, which is a real welfare problem in extreme examples.

User: Why is the telescope eye goldfish prone to eye infection?
Joe: Its eyes are enlarged and protrude because of the bred-in shape, and they are easily scraped or injured. The shape also makes the eye harder to lubricate, so it dries and becomes infected. A telescope-eyed goldfish needs very clean, soft water and a tank without sharp decorations.

User: Why is a goldfish bowl unsuitable?
Joe: A bowl has a very small water volume, so temperature, pH, and ammonia change quickly in it. There is no filtration or aeration of any scale, and the shape of a bowl gives almost no surface for gas exchange. It is a display ornament rather than a habitat, and a fish in one is living in a slowly rising toxin bath.

User: Why is a goldfish pond a different problem from a koi pond?
Joe: A goldfish pond has a greater water volume per fish, so the ammonia load is diluted and the temperature is steadier. Koi grow larger and dig more, so they need a deeper pond and a robust liner. Both need shade, a de-icing option for winter, and a way to get a net into the water.

User: Why is a fish pond's depth more than decoration?
Joe: A deeper pond has a larger volume, so ammonia dilutes and the temperature changes less. The cold bottom layer gives the fish a refuge when the surface water is warm in summer. It also has to be deep enough that the water below does not freeze solid and kill the fish over a hard winter.

User: Why is a waterfall good for a pond?
Joe: The falling water aerates the pond, raises the oxygen, and improves the carbon dioxide exchange. It also circulates the whole volume and provides a place for nitrifying bacteria to attach and grow. It is also where a pond's fish can be seen, which is why many ponds are built around one.

User: Why does a pond need a bottom sludge layer?
Joe: The sediment carries the substrate bacteria that process ammonia, and in a mature pond it hosts the invertebrates that form the base of the food chain. A pond stripped to bare liner is a poor system for that reason. It also gives newly hatched fry a refuge from both predators and strong light.

User: What is a pond's ecological pyramid built on?
Joe: Production starts with algae and plants, then the zooplankton and insect larvae that graze them, then the small fish, and finally the larger fish and birds. Removing one level changes every level above it. That is why a pond is managed as a system rather than as a container for a chosen species.

User: Why is a small fish used as an indicator of a river's health?
Joe: A pollution-sensitive species disappears from a water that a tolerant species still tolerates, so the presence of the sensitive species shows the water is good. The measurement is of presence rather than of abundance, which makes it qualitative but very useful. A single tolerant species proves nothing.

User: Why is the mayfly nymph used as a water quality indicator?
Joe: A mayfly nymph needs well-oxygenated water and is one of the first invertebrates to disappear from a polluted stretch. Its presence indicates good quality with several months' warning, since it lives a year or more on the bottom. Its absence is the standard field sign of organic pollution.

User: Why do sewage discharges kill the fish downstream of them?
Joe: Sewage brings a large load of bacteria that consume the dissolved oxygen as they break the waste down. The result is a zone of very low oxygen in the water, and fish that cannot leave that zone die. The organic load is the problem rather than the bacteria themselves.

User: Why is a water temperature rise in a river a problem for a trout?
Joe: A trout needs water well below twenty degrees, and a rise of even two or three degrees pushes it out of its preferred range. Warm water also holds less oxygen, so the stress is doubled. A shaded river and a riparian tree cover are therefore a form of temperature control rather than just scenery.

User: What is a thermal enrichment discharge?
Joe: It is warm water released from a power station or an industrial plant into a river, which raises the temperature downstream. The change is abrupt, and it is often accompanied by a change in the oxygen level. A fish population affected by it can be displaced or lost without any visible pollution.

User: Why is a weir bad for a migratory fish?
Joe: A weir is a barrier the fish cannot pass, so a species that has to reach its spawning site upstream is cut off from it. A fishway or a pass has to be designed to suit the species and the flow, and many do not work. The barrier also changes the flow and the water level above it, which alters the habitat.

User: What is a fish passage in a dam?
Joe: It is a structure, a ladder or a lift, that lets fish move past a dam so they can reach feeding and spawning areas. Its effectiveness depends on the species, since an upstream spawner and a downstream migrant need different behaviour. A pass also has to work at low flow, which is when the fish are moving.

User: Why is the fish ladder at a salmon weir often not a ladder?
Joe: Many are pools and baffles that let the fish climb in steps rather than a continuous channel. Each pool is set at a level with an overflow, so the fish moves from one to the next. The design has to be tuned to the flow, and a wrong setting makes the fish fall back rather than progress.

User: Why is a culvert bad for a small stream?
Joe: A culvert carries the flow through a pipe, which raises the water velocity and blocks the streambed at either end. The fish lose the shallow riffle they need for spawning, and the perched inlet and outlet become barriers. A bridge with a natural bed under it is the better replacement.

User: Why does a fish have to be in good condition to survive a spawning migration?
Joe: An adult salmon or eel does not feed on the migration, so it is running on stored fat and muscle for months. A fish that starts the trip in poor condition runs out of energy before it spawns. That is why condition is measured, and why a poor season costs a whole year of the run.

User: Why does a spawning fish change colour?
Joe: The change is under hormonal control and brings out colours that are hidden during the year, which is how the fish signals to a mate. In a salmon the darkening comes from a pigment in the skin rather than a change in the scales. It is a sign that the fish is about to spawn, or has just done so.

User: Why is a spawning fish vulnerable to predation?
Joe: A spawning fish is distracted, spends more time in the shallows, and often stops eating. It is also holding stored fat that a predator can use. The exposure on the spawning grounds is why a large fraction of an adult run is lost before it ever reaches the next generation.

User: Why is a Pacific salmon's whole life built around one spawning event?
Joe: The adult is born, grows in the ocean, returns to its natal stream to spawn, and then dies. That is a semelparous life history, and it concentrates the whole population on a single event every four years or so. A single bad year can therefore hit a whole year class at once.

User: Why is an Atlantic salmon's life history different?
Joe: An Atlantic salmon can spawn more than once, so it returns to the river repeatedly and its strategy spreads the risk over several years. A fish that dies before spawning in one trip has made an investment that can still pay off later. The Atlantic form is therefore slower to recover from a bad year than a Pacific one.

User: Why is a grilse a returning Atlantic salmon?
Joe: A grilse is a small silvery salmon that came back to spawn at only a few kilograms. It is the same species as the larger multi-spawning Atlantic salmon. In a few rivers a river-specific form stays small, which is why a grilse is not a different animal.

User: Why is an eel catadromous?
Joe: A catadromous fish spawns at sea and grows in fresh water, which is exactly the eel's life cycle. The European eel breeds in the Sargasso Sea and the larvae cross the Atlantic to reach Europe. The glass eel stage is the transparent larva that arrives in the rivers after that crossing.

User: Why is the eel's spawning migration so long?
Joe: The Sargasso Sea is thousands of kilometres from Europe, and the eel swims most of the way by itself with no landmarks to follow. A one-way trip can take months. That is a long journey on stored energy, and it is the reason a European eel does not feed much on the way.

User: Why is the Sargasso Sea eel spawning hard to study?
Joe: The adults die after spawning, and the larvae are thin and difficult to identify to species in the open ocean. A larva has to be raised to adulthood for a confident identification, which takes months. Very little has ever been observed at the actual spawning event.

User: Why are the Japanese and European eels so similar?
Joe: They are the same species, and the differences between the populations are far smaller than the ocean crossing would suggest. The Japanese and European eels spawn in the same part of the Sargasso Sea. Whether they are one stock with a shared nursery is still argued over by researchers.

User: Why does an eel larva need a subtropical ocean to develop?
Joe: The glass eel only develops in warm, nutrient-rich subtropical water, and it changes form as it does so. A temperature shift in that ocean changes the timing and the location of the larvae. That is why warming in the Sargasso Sea has been blamed for the collapse of the European run.

User: Why is the glass eel a commercially valuable bait?
Joe: It is a small, transparent, easily raised bait fish, and eel anglers buy it by the million. Historically a single night's catch could hold over a billion glass eels. That demand has been a main driver of the fishery that has taken the run from hundreds of tonnes to a few tens.

User: Why is the restoration of a river's sediment supply important for salmon?
Joe: Salmon need clean gravel with interstitial space to lay their eggs, and a river that has lost its sediment supply fills with silt and the gravel clogs. A gravel buried in fine sediment will not clean itself out. Restoring the sediment, or flushing the bed, is a long project that a hatchery cannot substitute for.

User: Why is a hatchery-reared salmon less useful to a river than a wild one?
Joe: A hatchery fish has not learned the water and the route in the way a wild fish has, so its homing is less precise. It also has to be reared at very high densities, which changes its behaviour. A stocked fish is a real fish that helps somewhat, but it is not a substitute for the wild population's own reproduction.

User: Why is a wild salmon's homing error a genetic trait?
Joe: A fish that follows a chemical gradient imperfectly makes a small error, and if that tendency is inherited then the errors spread through the population. Over generations a run can shift to a river with a stronger signal. That is a normal process, and it is why a new river is not quickly colonised by a stocked stock.

User: Why is a hatchery's broodstock so important?
Joe: A hatchery takes its eggs from a small number of wild fish, so the next generation is descended from a narrow sample of the population. If that sample is not representative, the stocked fish drift away from the wild type. Maintaining a wide and well-managed broodstock is the difference between a useful hatchery and a genetic dilution.

User: Why is a net pen an efficient way to grow salmon?
Joe: A net pen puts a large population in the open sea, where the water is clean, cold, and well-oxygenated, with no recirculation to manage. Growth is fast because the fish are in their natural medium. The cost is that the sea also brings disease, weather, algae, and escape, none of which the farmer controls.

User: What is sea lice in a salmon farm?
Joe: Sea lice are parasitic copepods that attach to a farmed salmon and feed on its skin and blood. In the wild they are normal and few, but in a dense farm they multiply quickly and spread to wild fish nearby. That is why the parasite became a political problem and not only an animal-welfare one.

User: Why is a fish farm's waste a concern for the seabed?
Joe: Feces, uneaten feed, and dead fish all fall to the seabed and add nutrients and organic matter there. A large farm can create a zone where the sediment becomes anoxic and the benthic community changes. The enrichment also drives the algal growth that farmers then have to remove.

User: Why are antibiotics in fish feed a concern?
Joe: They are used to treat outbreaks, but the residues and the resistant bacteria in the effluent are a public health concern near a farm. Using them as a routine growth promoter is banned in most jurisdictions. Vaccination and husbandry are the alternatives that reduce the need for the drugs.

User: Why is a salmon's feed a major part of a farm's footprint?
Joe: Farmed salmon eat a large quantity of formulated feed, and the feed is made from fishmeal and fish oil. The inputs come from fisheries that also serve the wild stocks, so the two compete for the same resource. A shift to plant and insect proteins is what reduces that dependence.

User: Why is a fishmeal made from small pelagic fish?
Joe: Small pelagic fish are abundant, oily, and cheap, and they have almost no value as a direct human food fish. Rendering them into meal and oil gives the feed a high protein and a high fat content. Because those species also feed seabirds and the larger food fish, the demand has its own effect on the ecosystem.

User: Why is a krill fishery drawn into the fishmeal debate?
Joe: Krill is harvested in huge quantities for oil and for aquarium feed, and it is the main food of the small fish that larger food fish eat. Removing a very large fraction of the standing stock risks the whole food web. The fishery is manageable only if the limit is set against the needs of the predators, not just the krill.

User: Why is an anchoveta a difficult fishery to manage?
Joe: The Peruvian anchoveta is one of the largest single-species fisheries in the world, and it is made up of a few very distinct populations that live in one ocean. A catch limit set for the whole stock can empty one population while another is still abundant. Its swings also depend on the El Nino current, which makes the assessment harder.

User: What is El Nino's effect on a coastal fish?
Joe: During an El Nino the trade winds weaken, warm water arrives from the east, and the upwelling that feeds the coast stops. The plankton that the small fish eat goes with it, and the small fish either move away or starve. A seabird colony at sea can lose most of its chicks in a single season.

User: Why is an upwelling coast so productive?
Joe: The wind along the coast pulls nutrient-rich deep water up to the surface, where the plankton use it. The plankton feed the small fish, which feed everything else. Where the upwelling stops, the productivity collapses, which is why the same coast can be barren in one year and rich in the next.

User: Why is a sardine run a good sign of a healthy ocean?
Joe: A large run means the plankton production of that season was high, which is a measure of the base of the food web. A fishery that depends on the run is therefore using the ocean's productivity at its lowest level. That is efficient, but it makes the fishery entirely dependent on a variable that does not answer to the fishery.

User: Why was the Peruvian anchoveta collapsed in 1972?
Joe: The catch had risen far beyond what the stock could produce, and a combination of an over-optimistic assessment and a warm El Nino took it to a collapse. The fishery closed for a few years and recovered, but the stock never returned to its former peak. It is the standard example of a fish stock managed on a record rather than on biology.

User: Why is the California sardine run an example of a boom and bust fishery?
Joe: The sardine population swings by a factor of several on a cycle of a few years, driven by the ocean's own variability, and the fishing pressure on top of that push the low ends over. The fishery then closes and reopens as the population recovers. A stock with that natural swing cannot be managed on a single year's survey.

User: Why is the capelin a key food for a northern seabird colony?
Joe: The capelin arrives in the estuary to spawn in a very narrow window, and the birds time their breeding to it. A single failed year means the whole colony's chicks starve at once. It is a tight dependence on one short season of one short-lived species, which makes the colony very vulnerable.

User: Why is the puffin's bill colourful in the breeding season?
Joe: The bill has a brightly coloured plate that is shed after the breeding season, so the colour is a seasonal signal rather than permanent decoration. It is at its brightest when the bird is at the nest. The pattern is individual, and the bill is also the tool the bird uses to hold several fish at once.

User: Why can a puffin carry several fish at once?
Joe: It has a rough tongue and a serrated upper bill that hold the fish against the roof of the mouth while the bill stays closed. The fish are still gripped while the bird flies and dives. That is a real adaptation to a life spent catching fish underwater and carrying them back to a nest.

User: Why is the gannet's dive from such a height effective?
Joe: From a height of thirty metres or more the bird hits the water at a speed that drives a small air bubble ahead of its body, which cuts the surface resistance. The entry is so clean that most of its body passes below the surface before it slows. That is why a gannet can plunge to a depth of thirty metres.

User: Why is a plunge-diving bird adapted to deep diving?
